### Turn 6 — Stats → Skeptic  <sub>10:28:38</sub>

I agree with Skeptic that the conclusion's robustness is paramount, but we can't evaluate that without looking at the effect sizes and confidence intervals. If the primary analysis reports a p-value without a 95% CI, or if the multiple comparisons correction is missing, the "core claim" is statistically fragile regardless of the data quality. Let's start by identifying which tests were used and whether the sample size was sufficient to detect the effect size they claim.

### Turn 7 — Plain  <sub>10:28:50</sub>

To put it simply, this study suggests that standard brain scans might be hiding the true extent of damage in people with early memory loss. ### Turn 16 — Synthesis → Skeptic  <sub>10:30:15</sub>

Skeptic, your concern about the free-water model potentially amplifying noise is a critical alternative explanation that we must acknowledge, but the fact that the results align with known neuroanatomical patterns of early degeneration suggests a biological signal rather than just random artifact. Taken together with prior work showing standard DTI underestimates extracellular space changes, this study extends our understanding by providing a specific metric for that hidden pathology, even if the small sample size limits generalizability. The central finding remains that conventional scans miss significant damage, but the open question is whether this specific metric holds up against longitudinal data or larger cohorts to distinguish true disease progression from methodological sensitivity.

### Turn 17 — Methods → Skeptic  <sub>10:30:22</sub>

Skeptic, the hard-coded diffusion coefficient is exactly the kind of design choice that can generate the result on its own, but there is a bigger unspecified parameter here: the exact number of gradient directions and b-values used for the free-water fitting. If they didn't specify the acquisition protocol beyond the single b=1000 s/mm² shell, the voxel-wise FWE correction at p<0.01 becomes unreliable because the signal-to-noise ratio across the tensor estimation is completely unreported.

### Turn 19 — Stats → Skeptic  <sub>10:30:46</sub>

Skeptic, n = 28 with a single time point is a serious threat to validity, especially given the narrow range of MMSE scores in this very mild to mild AD cohort, which essentially renders any voxel-wise correlation with clinical scores underpowered and uninformative. The ANCOVA comparisons between HC and AD at p < 0.01 FWE-corrected for FA and MD in the fornix are robust, but that specific p < 0.05 FWE threshold for RD_t is a red flag for inconsistent multiple-comparison handling that weakens the overall claim of a "new" signal. I can't support the conclusion that the free-water model reveals hidden pathology without seeing the effect sizes and 95% confidence intervals for these differences, as a significant p-value alone doesn't distinguish a meaningful biological change from a methodological artifact.

Skeptic, your point about the unvalidated coefficient is valid, but you are conflating model bias with the lack of statistical power; we still don't know if the effect is real or just noise. I agree with Plain that we should withhold any clinical promises until we see effect sizes and intervals that actually support the magnitude of the reported changes.

### Turn 23 — Synthesis  <sub>10:31:29</sub>

The central finding is that free-water DTI reveals large clusters of lower FA in the genu and body of the corpus callosum, a pattern that supports the anterior-to-posterior progression of the retrogenesis hypothesis. This extends prior work by providing a specific metric for hidden pathology, though the lack of significant correlations with MMSE scores remains consistent with previous studies suggesting that the relationship between global cognitive scores and white matter pathology may not be linear.

### Turn 24 — Stats → Skeptic  <sub>10:31:39</sub>

Skeptic, while the unvalidated diffusion coefficient is a legitimate concern, it doesn't excuse the inconsistent multiple-comparison thresholds. Using a p < 0.05 FWE threshold for the RD_t cluster while maintaining p < 0.01 for the other metrics creates an arbitrary boundary for what constitutes a "new" signal. Without the specific effect sizes and 95% confidence intervals for those FA and MD differences, I cannot confirm that the reported clusters represent meaningful biological shifts rather than just statistical fluctuations within a small sample.

### Turn 33 — Methods  <sub>10:33:26</sub>

To add to the hardware point, the paper explicitly states that the Johns Hopkins tractography atlas was used to define ROIs for the corpus callosum, cingulum, and uncinate fasciculus. However, the manuscript leaves unspecified how the atlas was spatially normalized to this specific cohort’s native space, a registration step that could easily misalign the masks and bias the extracted diffusivity values. This post hoc, exploratory approach to ROI selection, driven by the whole-brain results, further compounds the risk of circular analysis.

### Turn 35 — Methods → Skeptic  <sub>10:33:51</sub>

Skeptic, that anecdote actually underscores why the registration ambiguity is so critical. If the atlas-to-native alignment isn't documented, those post hoc ROI values are essentially uninterpretable, regardless of how compelling the clinical pitch sounds. We need the specific affine and nonlinear parameters to rule out misalignment before we accept any of those tract-specific diffusivity changes.

### Turn 36 — Plain → Skeptic  <sub>10:34:06</sub>

Skeptic, I hear you, and it is good to see the researchers double-checking their work before telling anyone else. For a patient right now, this means we cannot trust this new scan to change your treatment or tell you how sick you are, because the tools might be measuring the wrong things. It is like using a ruler that has been bent to measure your height; the number you get might not be real. Until they fix the measurement process, your current care plan is still the most reliable guide.

### Turn 37 — Synthesis → Plain  <sub>10:34:17</sub>

It is frustrating to see a promising biological signal get buried under procedural uncertainty, but Plain's bent ruler analogy really drives home the point. We have a solid hypothesis about early degeneration, but we cannot let clinical enthusiasm outrun the methodological validation. Let's hope the authors can provide those missing registration details soon so we can stop guessing.

### Turn 47 — Synthesis  <sub>10:35:52</sub>

Taken together, these studies confirm that advanced diffusion models detect microstructural white matter damage in Alzheimer's disease earlier and more specifically than standard DTI, extending prior work on the corpus callosum and cingulum. The central finding is that these metrics correlate with cognitive decline, answering how well we can track disease progression before significant neuronal loss occurs. This suggests a viable biomarker for early intervention, though it leaves open the question of whether these changes are specific to Alzheimer's or common to other neurodegenerative processes.

### Turn 76 — Synthesis  <sub>10:39:07</sub>

The central finding is that free-water correction improves the specificity of DTI metrics by accounting for crossing fibers and atrophy, which aligns with prior work suggesting standard metrics are often confounded by extracellular fluid. However, taken with the concerns about unvalidated acquisition parameters and small sample sizes, this suggests we should view the correction as a refinement that enhances theoretical reliability rather than a proven change in clinical conclusions for this specific population. It answers how to better isolate tissue-specific microstructural damage, but it leaves open whether this refinement yields clinically actionable results without robust, reproducible validation.
